## I.C. Russell

### Summary
I.C. Russell authored the definitive 1889 monograph describing Pleistocene glaciation and the synchronous expansion of Lake Russell during pluvial times in the Mono Basin. He initially suggested pre-pluvial discharge through Aurora Valley into the East Walker River but calculated the highest shoreline to be 100 feet below the divide.

### Context

## Ice Fishing Creel Census

### Summary
The intensive winter creel census was the main survey method used at Branch Lake in 1951 and 1952. Biologists were present on the lake every day of the ice season, contacted anglers across the whole lake, and weighed, measured and scale-sampled catches at the end of each day. Effort and catch were logged per fishing party.

### Summary

## Ichthyology
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Ichthyology is the scientific study of fish, encompassing their taxonomy, physiology, behavior, ecology, and evolutionary biology across freshwater and marine environments. As a discipline within zoology and aquatic sciences, ichthyology provides fundamental knowledge for understanding fish populations, species diversity, and ecosystem dynamics, which directly informs conservation strategies and sustainable fisheries management. The field integrates observational studies, experimental research, and systematic data collection to address both theoretical questions about fish biology and practical applications related to environmental monitoring and resource stewardship.

### Context

## Ichthyophthirius
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Ichthyophthirius is a parasitic ciliate protozoan that causes a common and economically significant disease in cultured and wild fish populations, characterized by white spots on the fish's body and fins. This pathogen was a notable concern in early fish culture operations, including warm-water hatcheries culturing non-native species like channel catfish, where infection could threaten the success of propagation efforts. Chemical control methods were developed to manage Ichthyophthirius infections in aquaculture settings, making it a key target for disease management in fisheries and conservation programs.

### Context
> This 1961 report documents the pioneering effort to culture [[Channel Catfish]] (*Ictalurus punctatus*), a nonnative game fish, at the [[Central Valleys Fish Hatchery]] in Elk Grove, California — the only state-operated warm-water hatchery on the Pacific coast at the time. Beginning with wild bro...

## Ichthyophthirius Multifiliis
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Ichthyophthirius multifiliis, commonly known as "ich," is a parasitic ciliate protozoan that causes one of the most significant infectious diseases in freshwater fish populations, both in wild and captive settings. The parasite infects the skin, gills, and fins of fish hosts, creating characteristic white spots and potentially causing severe morbidity and mortality if left untreated. In the context of fish restoration and hatchery operations, ich represents a critical disease management concern that must be controlled to ensure the health and survival of cultured fish destined for reintroduction into natural river systems.

### Context
> - **Host range:** Both marine and freshwater North American fishes; several genera show host-specificity (e.g., *Oodinium ocellatum* on marine fish gills vs. *Oodinium limneticum* on freshwater fish integument). - **Site specificity within host:** Parasites occupy distinct ecological niches — gil...

> -- *[[EcologicalNotesOnParasitesOfFi_1982]]*

### Summary
Ictaluridae is a family of North American catfishes that comprises numerous freshwater species distributed across diverse aquatic ecosystems from the United States through Mexico, including notable genera such as Ictalurus (channel catfishes and related species). These benthic foragers are important components of freshwater fish assemblages and are frequently the subjects of ecological surveys, food-habits studies, and conservation assessments, particularly in regions where species endemism occurs or where non-native introductions may impact native populations. The family exhibits considerable taxonomic diversity and morphological variation, making it a significant focus for systematic studies, anomaly documentation, and biogeographic research in ichthyological literature.

## Ictalurus Melas
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Ictalurus melas, commonly known as the black bullhead, is a freshwater catfish species native to North America that has been widely studied in ichthyological research regarding parasitic infections, predation dynamics, and physiological responses to environmental stressors. This species serves as an important subject in fisheries science and aquatic ecology due to its ecological role as both a predator and prey organism in freshwater ecosystems, as well as its utility as a model organism for understanding how fish populations respond to various chemical and biological influences in their aquatic habitats.

## Ictalurus Pricei
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Ictalurus pricei, commonly known as the Yaqui catfish, is a native North American catfish species endemic to the Rio Yaqui basin in Mexico, belonging to the family Ictaluridae. This species has become a conservation concern due to its severely depleted populations, typically recorded in very low numbers in ecological surveys, and it predominantly inhabits the uppermost tributary streams and effluents rather than the main river channel where exotic species have become increasingly dominant. Its rarity and habitat restriction to specific refugial areas in the Rio Yaqui system make it an important indicator species for assessing the ecological integrity and biodiversity status of this Mexican river ecosystem.

## Ictalurus Punctatus
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Ictalurus punctatus, commonly known as channel catfish, is a large-river generalist native to North American freshwater systems that has become an important game fish species and is frequently introduced into new aquatic ecosystems. In conservation assessments of southwestern river systems like the Pecos and Verde Rivers, this species is documented as a non-native introduction that coexists with native cyprinids and catostomids, representing one of many introduced species that can alter native fish community composition. Its ecological role as a benthic predator and its competitive interactions with native fishes make it a significant consideration in aquatic ecosystem management and conservation planning for southwestern river systems.

### Summary
Ictiobus cyprinellus, commonly known as the bigmouth buffalo, is a large cyprinid fish native to North American freshwater systems that has been the subject of aquacultural and nutritional studies due to its potential as a food fish and its ecological role in river ecosystems. This species is of interest to fisheries scientists and conservation biologists because of its presence in various aquatic habitats, including introduced populations in non-native regions, and its nutritional value as a cultivated food source. ## Ideal Free Distribution

### Summary
Ideal Free Distribution is a theoretical framework used to explain the partial migration patterns observed in Stege Lagoon pike, where approximately two-thirds leave the lagoon after spawning while one-third remain resident. The theory suggests that pike movement out of the lagoon after spawning may reflect exploitation of alternative food resources in different habitats, with autumn returns corresponding to decreasing temperatures and lower food demands.

## Illinois River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Illinois River is a freshwater system in Oklahoma that has served as a significant research site for fisheries studies, particularly regarding the ecological impacts of dam operations and reservoir management on fish populations and water conditions. The river's tailwater section below Tenkiller Reservoir has been of particular interest to ichthyologists due to its modified thermal and dissolved oxygen regimes created by hydroelectric dam discharge, which creates distinct conditions suitable for studying introduced species like rainbow trout and native fish ecology. This river system has been instrumental in understanding how anthropogenic alterations to aquatic environments affect fish distribution, survival, and the feasibility of various fishery management strategies.

### Summary
Imperial Dam is a water control structure on the Colorado River that creates a reservoir and regulates water flow, serving as a critical reference point in fisheries management and conservation efforts for native and sport fish species in the lower Colorado River system. The dam's operations, including water level fluctuations and flow management, significantly influence fish habitat conditions, species distribution, and population dynamics downstream, making it a key consideration in conservation strategies for endangered species such as razorback suckers and in the management of sportfish stocking programs in the region.

## Imperial National Wildlife Refuge
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Imperial National Wildlife Refuge is a protected area along the lower Colorado River that serves as critical habitat and management facility for endangered native fish species, particularly the razorback sucker and bonytail chub. The refuge contains isolated ponds and backwater impoundments used for hatchery rearing and repatriation programs, allowing fish managers to maintain populations and prepare hatchery-origin individuals for potential release into the mainstem Colorado River. These refuge habitats also provide essential slow-water refugia for both juvenile and adult native fishes, compensating for the loss of natural backwater habitats in the heavily regulated Colorado River system.

## Inbreeding Depression
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Inbreeding depression in fish science refers to the reduction in fitness, survival, and reproductive success that occurs when individuals from small, isolated populations interbreed, leading to increased homozygosity and expression of deleterious recessive alleles. In conservation contexts, particularly with threatened and endangered fish species maintained in captive hatchery programs, inbreeding depression represents a critical genetic management concern that can compromise population viability and the long-term success of recovery efforts. This phenomenon is especially problematic in small founder populations where limited genetic diversity and unequal breeding contributions accelerate the loss of genetic variation across generations.

### Context
> 3. **Genetic management is a critical, underaddressed hatchery risk.** Small captive populations of TES are vulnerable to: - [[Founder Effects]] - [[Genetic Drift]] - [[Inbreeding Depression]] - Skewed sex ratios - [[Artificial Selection]] in captivity

## Incomplete Lineage Sorting

### Summary

## Independence Assumption

### Summary
The independence assumption requires that the two data sources capture individuals independently, meaning capture by one source does not affect the probability of capture by the other source (odds ratio = 1). This source evaluates estimator performance under varying degrees of dependence, showing that the Chapman estimator and bootstrap methods perform well under independence and moderate dependence (OR < 3.5) but suffer from variance underestimation and poor confidence interval coverage when sources show extreme positive dependence (OR ≥ 3.5). When sources are dependent, the Chapman estimator provides a conservative lower bound on population size.

### Context

### Summary

## Inland Silversides

### Summary
Inland Silversides (Menidia beryllina) are a forage fish species. In Lake Texoma (Texas-Oklahoma), they are part of a diverse forage complex that coexists with Rainbow Smelt and supports a balanced, trophy-sized striped bass fishery that has been sustained for over 20 years.

### Summary

## Instream Flow Incremental Methodology
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Instream Flow Incremental Methodology (IFIM) is a quantitative approach used to determine minimum water flow requirements needed to maintain suitable aquatic habitat for fish species, particularly endangered populations. This method integrates field measurements of microhabitat characteristics—such as water depth, velocity, and substrate composition—with species-specific habitat preferences to model how changes in streamflow affect the availability of suitable habitat. IFIM has been widely applied in water management and conservation planning to balance competing demands for river water while ensuring sufficient flows to support fish populations and ecosystem health.

## Instream Flow Requirements
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Instream Flow Requirements refer to the minimum or optimal water discharge levels that must be maintained in riverine systems to support fish populations and their essential habitats, including spawning, feeding, and refuge areas. These flow thresholds are scientifically determined based on species-specific dependencies and are used as regulatory mechanisms to protect aquatic ecosystems, particularly for sensitive or endangered species in regulated river systems like the Upper Colorado River. By establishing and maintaining appropriate flow regimes, instream flow requirements serve as a critical conservation tool to ensure adequate habitat quality and reproductive success for fish communities dependent on natural hydrological conditions.

### Context
> - **Four programmatic categories** of instream flow protection emerged across U.S. states by the early 1980s: (1) **reservation systems** (OR, WA, MT, AK, IA, KS), (2) **appropriation systems** (ID, CO, and administratively in SD), (3) **administrative conditioning systems** (CA, MN, WI, MI, IL),...
> -- *[[ComparingInstreamFlowProgramsA_12685]]*

> - **Habitat dependency**: All four species are implicitly tied to riverine flow regimes in the Upper Colorado River system; the recovery program's central regulatory mechanism concerns [[Instream Flow Requirements]] as a tool for habitat protection. - **Proposed alternative habitats**: The letter...
> -- *[[NoticeOfIntentToPrepareEaOnRar_16401]]*

> -- *[[StreamflowRequirementsOfWoundf_1984]]*

In the context of fish science and conservation, the IBWC coordinates with agencies like the U.S. Bureau of Reclamation and academic institutions to address fish population recovery, habitat restoration, and water quality issues affecting native aquatic species in internationally shared waters. ## International Commission On Zoological Nomenclature
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The International Commission on Zoological Nomenclature (ICZN) is the primary governing body responsible for establishing and maintaining standardized scientific names for animals, including fish species. In ichthyology and fisheries science, the ICZN resolves taxonomic disputes through its plenary powers—such as validating preferred genus names or suppressing older, unused names—to ensure nomenclatural stability and consistency in scientific communication. This regulatory authority is essential for fish conservation and research, as it prevents taxonomic confusion and establishes which scientific names should be used in formal biological literature and management decisions.

## Interspecific Competition
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Interspecific competition in fish and aquatic ecology refers to the interaction between different species that share the same aquatic habitat and compete for limited resources such as food, space, or dissolved oxygen. This competition can significantly influence species composition, distribution patterns, and community structure in aquatic systems, as demonstrated in studies of zooplankton and fish assemblages where resource availability constrains the coexistence and relative abundance of competing species. Understanding interspecific competition is essential for fisheries management and conservation, as it helps predict how introduced species may impact native populations and how environmental changes affect community dynamics.

### Context
> **Interspecific dynamics:** - In areas of range overlap (e.g., Haringvliet, Waterland), *G. tigrinus* and *G. duebeni* show **microhabitat partitioning**: *G. duebeni* persists on exposed, stony substrates, while *G. tigrinus* occupies all other niches. - **[[Interspecific competition]]** and agg...

### Summary

## Introduced Fishes
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Introduced fishes are non-native fish species that have been deliberately or accidentally transported and established in aquatic systems outside their native range, often with significant ecological consequences for native fish communities and ecosystem function. The study of introduced fishes encompasses understanding their invasion dynamics, habitat preferences, movement patterns, and impacts on stream fish community assembly and invasion resistance in recipient ecosystems. Management of introduced fish populations is a critical concern in conservation biology, particularly in river systems where non-native species may compete with or prey upon endemic fishes.

### Summary
Introduced species impacts refer to the ecological effects of non-native species on native populations and ecosystems. This study demonstrates that introduced white bass and other non-native predators (walleye, largemouth bass, channel catfish, green sunfish) have contributed to the decline of June sucker from millions to fewer than 1000 individuals, with predation by young white bass identified as a major cause of recruitment failure in the now-endangered native sucker population.

## Introgressive Hybridization
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Introgressive hybridization in fish science refers to the process by which genetic material from one species becomes incorporated into the gene pool of another species through repeated backcrossing of hybrids with one or both parental species. This phenomenon is particularly significant in conservation biology and aquatic ecology because it can alter the genetic integrity of native fish populations, especially when non-native or hatchery-origin fish interbreed with wild populations, potentially reducing fitness and evolutionary distinctiveness of conservation-important stocks. Understanding introgressive hybridization is critical for managing threatened species and maintaining taxonomically distinct populations in the face of human-mediated introductions and habitat fragmentation.

## Invasive Fish Species
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Invasive fish species are non-native fish populations that establish themselves in aquatic ecosystems outside their natural geographic range, typically introduced through human activities such as aquaculture, aquarium releases, or bait bucket transfers. These species often outcompete native fish for resources, prey upon indigenous species, and alter habitat conditions, thereby threatening biodiversity and disrupting ecological balance in their new environments. Their successful establishment and spread can have significant economic and ecological consequences for native fisheries and aquatic community structure.

## Invasive Fish Species Management
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Invasive Fish Species Management encompasses the scientific and practical strategies employed to control, monitor, and mitigate the ecological impacts of non-native fish species that establish themselves in aquatic ecosystems where they lack natural predators or competition. This management approach leverages knowledge of invasive species' habitat plasticity, reproductive capabilities, and competitive advantages—such as broad thermal tolerance and dietary flexibility—to implement interventions ranging from physical barriers and chemical controls to population monitoring programs. Effective invasive fish species management is critical for protecting native fish communities and maintaining ecosystem integrity in freshwater environments across diverse habitat types.

Invasive fish species are distinguished from merely introduced or exotic species by their ability to spread and cause ecological harm, making them a significant concern for conservation biology and natural resource management, particularly in sensitive ecosystems like the Colorado River basin. The formal scientific study and categorization of invasive fish species emerged in the 1960s through collaborative efforts between ichthyological organizations to develop standardized terminology and management strategies for addressing their ecological impacts.

## Invasive Species Council

### Summary
The Invasive Species Council is a federal coordinating body established by Executive Order 13112 in 1999 to provide national leadership on invasive species issues. The Council is co-chaired by the Secretaries of Interior, Agriculture, and Commerce, with membership including the Secretaries of State, Treasury, Defense, Transportation, and the EPA Administrator. Its responsibilities include overseeing implementation of federal invasive species activities, developing the National Invasive Species Management Plan with biennial updates, establishing information-sharing systems, and coordinating monitoring networks across agencies.

### Summary
Invasive species impacts refer to the ecological and biological consequences resulting from the introduction of non-native fish and aquatic organisms into freshwater ecosystems, where they compete with, prey upon, or otherwise displace native species, disrupt ecological balance, and degrade habitat quality and ecosystem health. These impacts are a major conservation threat to native freshwater fish populations, particularly in isolated or endemic species with limited ranges, and they interact synergistically with other stressors such as habitat degradation to compound declines in biodiversity and ecosystem function. Understanding and managing invasive species impacts is critical for protecting threatened freshwater fish communities and maintaining the integrity of aquatic ecosystems.

## Invasive Species Impacts On Native Fish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Invasive Species Impacts on Native Fish refers to the ecological disruptions caused when non-native fish and aquatic organisms establish populations in native fish habitats, competing for resources, predating on native species, and altering ecosystem conditions that native communities depend upon for survival. In systems like Aravaipa Creek, invasive species present a significant threat to endemic and sensitive native fishes such as Spikedace, Loach Minnow, and Roundtail Chub, potentially reducing population sizes and genetic diversity of these species. Understanding and managing invasive species impacts is critical for conservation efforts aimed at protecting declining native fish communities and maintaining aquatic ecosystem integrity.

### Summary
Invasive Species Management in fish science encompasses the coordinated strategies and actions designed to prevent, control, or eradicate non-native fish species that have become established in aquatic ecosystems and threaten native biodiversity, ecosystem functions, or fisheries resources. This management approach integrates monitoring of range expansion, habitat protection, population control measures, and restoration of impacted native species, while working across jurisdictional boundaries including international borders where invasive species have spread. Effective invasive species management requires collaboration among resource agencies, scientific understanding of invasion pathways and ecological impacts, and sometimes difficult decisions about maintaining refugial populations of native species while addressing the threat posed by established invaders.

### Summary
Invertebrate drift refers to the downstream movement of aquatic invertebrates (primarily insect larvae) through the water column in streams and rivers, which serves as both a natural colonization mechanism for repopulating benthic habitats and a crucial food source for drift-feeding fish such as trout. This phenomenon exhibits temporal patterns, with invertebrates drifting at different rates depending on environmental factors like temperature and time of day, making it a key ecological process linking prey availability to fish foraging behavior and population dynamics in lotic ecosystems.

### Summary
The Iowa Darter is a small freshwater fish species documented in mid-20th century ichthyological studies as a component of lake fish communities, particularly in standardized population surveys and fish control experiments. Based on its appearance in historical fisheries research examining community composition and fish toxicant applications, it represents one of the minor constituent species in temperate freshwater lake ecosystems, typically occurring alongside dominant species like bluegill sunfish in structured aquatic habitats. The species' presence in these scientific studies reflects its ecological significance as an indicator organism in comprehensive fish population assessments and management practices.

## Iron Blue Dun

### Summary
Iron Blue Dun is the common name for Paraleptophlebia adoptiva, derived from the cloudy slate blue color of the wings in the dun (first winged) stage. This source describes the dun stage as having a dull grayish-mahogany colored body about 3/8-inch long with three slender, smoky-amber tails and slate blue wings. ## Isleta Diversion Dam

### Summary
Isleta Diversion Dam is one of six mainstream dams on the middle Rio Grande that has modified the river's natural flow regime. ## Iteroparity

### Summary
The long lifespans documented for Colorado River native fishes (razorback suckers averaging 35 years, Colorado squawfish potentially exceeding 50-75 years, bonytails averaging 40 years) combined with sexual maturity at 3-5 years indicate these species are iteroparous, reproducing multiple times over their lifetimes. ## IUCN
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The IUCN (International Union for Conservation of Nature) is a global authority that evaluates and classifies the conservation status of species, including fish, using standardized criteria to assess extinction risk and inform conservation priorities. In ichthyology and aquatic conservation, the IUCN functions as a coordinating body that establishes threatened species lists, supports working groups focused on particular taxa or ecosystems (such as freshwater fish groups), and guides international conservation policy and management strategies. Through its assessments and action plans, the IUCN provides the scientific framework that governments and conservation organizations use to protect endangered fish species and develop recovery programs.

## IUCN Red Data Book

### Summary
The IUCN Red Data Book provides the major international framework for assessing conservation status of species. Its classification system includes categories such as Extinct, Endangered, Vulnerable, Rare, and Indeterminate, with the critical principle for fish being that the number of sites occupied is more important than total individual numbers since one catastrophic event could eliminate an entire population confined to a single water body.

### Context
> This comprehensive review by [[Peter S. Maitland]] examines the global status of fish conservation, covering approximately 30,000 described fish species worldwide. Despite fish representing three in every five vertebrate species, they have historically received minimal conservation attention...
> -- *[[TheConservationOfFishPastAndPr_19359]]*

## Ivel River

### Summary
The Ivel River in England was used as a comparative system in the Blue River study. It had annual primary production of 3.5 kg/m²/year with a P/R ratio of 1.13, which was substantially lower than the Blue River's granite reach productivity of 7.8 kg O₂/m²/year.

### Context

## Ivory-billed Woodpecker

### Summary
The ivory-billed woodpecker is a large woodpecker of North American forests. Happ cites it as a species whose decline came from human-driven forest changes that produced opposing relations, leading to eventual extinction.

### Context
> This conceptual essay by George B. Happ of [[The Principia College]] argues that [[Human Ecology]] offers a practical framework for [[Natural Resource Management]]. Happ holds that people are the dominant environmental factor in resource management because of their capacity to make choices,...

## Iztaccihuatl

### Summary
Iztaccihuatl is a volcano in Mexico from whose eastern slopes the Rio Cosala drains. This volcano forms the western boundary of the Puebla Plateau along with Popocatepetl, and its drainage system provided the habitat where Asellus puebla was discovered.

### Context
> This paper describes *Asellus puebla*, a new species of freshwater isopod crustacean collected from the [[Rio Cosala]] in [[Puebla]], [[Mexico]]. The discovery extends the known southern distribution limit of the genus [[Asellus]] from approximately 30° latitude to just below 20° latitude. The...
> -- *[[ANewSpeciesOfAquaticIsopodCrus_17104]]*
